[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just… I can’t stop noticing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s in every photo, every reflection, even in windows when I’m walking by—actually, the glass in office buildings is the worst, which is ridiculous because I work around buildings all day.\n\nPeople keep telling me I look fine, but I don’t know how they don’t see it. It feels so obvious to me. And it’s gotten to where I’m avoiding presentations and meeting clients, which is a problem because that’s a big part of my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your reflection throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. Before work especially, or if we’re going to some family thing, I can lose hours in the bathroom mirror. I’ll check one side, then the other, then take pictures on my phone from different angles because the mirror feels like it lies sometimes, but then the photos look worse.\n\nAnd then I’ll redo my makeup, adjust my scarf, take it off, put it back on… my husband will be waiting by the door and I’ll ask him, “Does my face look lopsided today?” He always says no, but I don’t really believe him. Then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos online, comparing mine to theirs, which is not helpful, I know, but I still do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking rituals or will you feel anxious if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t think of it like a ritual exactly, but if I try not to check, I get really tense. Like before work, or if there’s a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to look once and leave, but then I’m in the bathroom for ages—checking the mirror, taking phone pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup, changing my scarf.\n\nAnd my husband will be waiting, and I’ll ask him again if my face looks lopsided, even though I already know what he’s going to say. Then sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing, which is stupid because it just makes me feel worse. But if I don’t do it, it’s like I can’t settle. I keep thinking, what if it really looks bad today and I didn’t catch it?\n\nDoctor: Is it only your face and jaw that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my face… the mouth and jaw area, that droop or unevenness. That’s the thing I can’t stop seeing. In photos, reflections, even like… walking past glass buildings, which I notice constantly because of work, and then I’m supposed to go into a meeting and act normal.\n\nI know people say I look fine, but it feels so obvious to me. Like clients must be looking at it when I’m talking. So I’ve started avoiding presentations when I can, or trying to stay out of client meetings. I don’t really focus on other parts of my body the same way—it’s this part of my face that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face could ever look perfectly even and symmetrical to you, or do you believe it's inherently flawed in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I think that’s the problem. Part of me keeps thinking if I just catch the right angle or fix my makeup the right way, maybe it’ll look even. But then every photo, every reflection, I see that droop around my mouth and jaw again.\n\nIt feels built in, like it’s just my face. And I know people say I look fine, but I can’t unsee it. I’ll be in a meeting, or even thinking about a presentation, and all I can imagine is clients staring at that uneven part while I’m talking. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients when I can. It just feels too obvious.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about other things being uneven or flawed besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I notice little things sometimes, like anyone does, but it doesn’t grab me the same way. It’s this droop around my mouth and my jaw, the unevenness there. I see it in every photo, every reflection, even when I’m just passing a window.\n\nAnd then I’m supposed to go meet a client and talk about a design like I’m not thinking the whole time that they’re staring at it. I know people say I look fine, my husband says it, but it just feels so obvious to me. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and client meetings when I can. It’s really that part of my face that I can’t stop seeing.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw affected your relationships with friends and family, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, it has. I try to tell myself it’s mainly work, but then it spills over. Like there was this awards thing—architectural awards, lots of people standing around with drinks and pretending not to judge each other’s shoes, you know—and I just couldn’t go. I kept thinking everyone would be looking at my face when I talked.\n\nAnd family gatherings… I’ve started missing those or leaving things to the last minute until it all falls apart. My husband gets frustrated because we’ll have plans and then I can’t make myself get ready, or I’ll keep checking my face and suddenly we’re late or not going.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I even cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute, which wasn’t family, I know, but it all feels connected. My deadlines are slipping, people are upset with me, and at home it’s like everything is revolving around whether I can stand being seen that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that even if someone proved to you that your face is symmetrical, you would still see it as uneven?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, even when people tell me it looks fine, or I try to convince myself a photo is just lighting, I still see that droop around my mouth and jaw. It’s like my eyes go straight to it.\n\nAnd I know symmetry is one of those things architects talk about all the time—balance, proportion, all that—and maybe that makes me worse about it, I don’t know. But if someone showed me measurements or something, I think I’d still look in the mirror and feel like, no, it’s uneven. It just feels so obvious to me that I can’t stop checking for it, and then I don’t want clients or people in presentations looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw started to affect your overall quality of life or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I keep thinking of it as “just” work, but it’s not. I mean, work is where it shows up in the biggest, most humiliating ways—like I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face being seen.\n\nAnd then there are these award-related networking things I’m supposed to go to, and I know they matter. You see people, you make contacts, you pretend to enjoy tiny appetizers… but I’ve been missing those too. I’ll get dressed and then start looking at my face and suddenly I can’t go.\n\nAnd it’s not staying neatly in one box. My project deadlines are slipping, and family gatherings are starting to fall apart because I either avoid them or make us late or just don’t go. It feels like everything gets planned around whether I can stand people looking at my face that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having intrusive thoughts or compulsions to repeat certain behaviors that are not related to your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really, I don’t think. I mean, unless you count the checking, but that’s obviously about my face. Other than that, I don’t think I have rituals or anything.\n\nI do get stuck on things sometimes, but I’ve always been particular—architecture sort of rewards that, you know, checking lines and details and whether something is level. My husband would probably say I fuss over things, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t noticed anything that feels separate from the face and jaw thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationship with your husband?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because it feels like everything is affected lately. My husband sees me getting ready and then not leaving, or making us late because I’m stuck looking at my face, and then I get upset and he doesn’t know what to say.\n\nIt’s not just at home, though—like, I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I couldn’t face being seen, and I’ve been missing those award networking events too. Those things sound work-related, but then I come home tense and ashamed, and it spills over. Project deadlines are slipping, family gatherings are falling apart… so yes, it’s definitely affecting us, even if I don’t always admit that to him.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other thoughts or worries that you feel are excessive or uncontrollable, outside of your concerns about your face and jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m trying to think. I mean, I worry about deadlines and whether clients are happy, but that feels like normal architect worry, if there is such a thing. Buildings have so many moving parts—permits, materials, people changing their minds after they’ve already approved something…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. Outside of my face and jaw, I don’t think there’s anything that feels that uncontrollable in the same way. I can get stressed, definitely, but I haven’t noticed some separate worry that takes over like this does.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life, besides your appearance, where you feel like you're excessively focused on details or flaws?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, in my work I notice flaws constantly—that’s part of the job, really. If a line is off or a material doesn’t sit right, it bothers me, but that feels… normal? Or at least normal for architects.\n\nMy husband jokes that I can spot a crooked picture frame from across a room, which is true, but I don’t think it takes over the way this does. Though now I’m thinking about this kitchen renovation we did years ago where the cabinet gaps were uneven and I kept pointing it out until everyone was sick of me—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… besides my appearance, I don’t know that there’s another area where it feels excessive in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your attention to detail at work is similar to how you focus on the details of your face?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but it’s different. At work, if I’m looking at a drawing or a model, the details have a purpose. Like, a line being off actually changes something—sorry, I’m thinking of this facade detail that drove me crazy once because the shadow gap was wrong.\n\nBut with my face, it’s like I can’t let it go. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo or reflection. Even when people say I look fine, it feels so obvious to me. And then I start thinking, how can I stand in front of clients like this? That’s when I avoid presentations or don’t want to meet people. So… no, it’s not really the same. It feels much more stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your focus on the details of your face is similar to how you focus on details in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s… similar in that I notice tiny things, I guess. Like if a line is off in a drawing, I’ll see it right away—actually there was this stair detail once that nobody else cared about and I couldn’t stop pointing at it, which probably drove everyone mad.\n\nBut my face feels different. That’s why I’m here, really. I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo, every reflection. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” it doesn’t land. It feels so obvious to me that I start thinking everyone must be noticing it, and then I don’t want to give presentations or meet clients. So… yes and no. The attention to detail is familiar, but this is much more consuming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be this bothered by small imperfections in other areas of your life if they weren't related to your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I can get annoyed by things—like if a tile line is off, or a drawing has some tiny mistake, and then I’ll think about it longer than maybe I should. My husband would say I’m impossible about that sort of thing.\n\nBut… I’m here because of my face. Because I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s in every photo, every reflection, even a dark window sometimes—sorry, that sounds dramatic, but it’s true. And when people tell me I look fine, it doesn’t really help, because to me it feels so obvious.\n\nWith other imperfections, I might be irritated. With this, I start avoiding presentations and clients. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It just feels too obvious.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about other things being uneven or flawed besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I notice little things sometimes, like anyone does, but it doesn’t grab me the same way. It’s this droop around my mouth and my jaw, the unevenness there. I see it in every photo, every reflection, even when I’m just passing a window.\n\nAnd then I’m supposed to go meet a client and talk about a design like I’m not thinking the whole time that they’re staring at it. I know people say I look fine, my husband says it, but it just feels so obvious to me. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and client meetings when I can. It’s really that part of my face that I can’t stop seeing.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw affected your relationships with friends and family, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, it has. I try to tell myself it’s mainly work, but then it spills over. Like there was this awards thing—architectural awards, lots of people standing around with drinks and pretending not to judge each other’s shoes, you know—and I just couldn’t go. I kept thinking everyone would be looking at my face when I talked.\n\nAnd family gatherings… I’ve started missing those or leaving things to the last minute until it all falls apart. My husband gets frustrated because we’ll have plans and then I can’t make myself get ready, or I’ll keep checking my face and suddenly we’re late or not going.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I even cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute, which wasn’t family, I know, but it all feels connected. My deadlines are slipping, people are upset with me, and at home it’s like everything is revolving around whether I can stand being seen that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that even if someone proved to you that your face is symmetrical, you would still see it as uneven?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, even when people tell me it looks fine, or I try to convince myself a photo is just lighting, I still see that droop around my mouth and jaw. It’s like my eyes go straight to it.\n\nAnd I know symmetry is one of those things architects talk about all the time—balance, proportion, all that—and maybe that makes me worse about it, I don’t know. But if someone showed me measurements or something, I think I’d still look in the mirror and feel like, no, it’s uneven. It just feels so obvious to me that I can’t stop checking for it, and then I don’t want clients or people in presentations looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw started to affect your overall quality of life or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I keep thinking of it as “just” work, but it’s not. I mean, work is where it shows up in the biggest, most humiliating ways—like I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face being seen.\n\nAnd then there are these award-related networking things I’m supposed to go to, and I know they matter. You see people, you make contacts, you pretend to enjoy tiny appetizers… but I’ve been missing those too. I’ll get dressed and then start looking at my face and suddenly I can’t go.\n\nAnd it’s not staying neatly in one box. My project deadlines are slipping, and family gatherings are starting to fall apart because I either avoid them or make us late or just don’t go. It feels like everything gets planned around whether I can stand people looking at my face that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having intrusive thoughts or compulsions to repeat certain behaviors that are not related to your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really, I don’t think. I mean, unless you count the checking, but that’s obviously about my face. Other than that, I don’t think I have rituals or anything.\n\nI do get stuck on things sometimes, but I’ve always been particular—architecture sort of rewards that, you know, checking lines and details and whether something is level. My husband would probably say I fuss over things, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t noticed anything that feels separate from the face and jaw thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationship with your husband?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because it feels like everything is affected lately. My husband sees me getting ready and then not leaving, or making us late because I’m stuck looking at my face, and then I get upset and he doesn’t know what to say.\n\nIt’s not just at home, though—like, I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I couldn’t face being seen, and I’ve been missing those award networking events too. Those things sound work-related, but then I come home tense and ashamed, and it spills over. Project deadlines are slipping, family gatherings are falling apart… so yes, it’s definitely affecting us, even if I don’t always admit that to him.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other thoughts or worries that you feel are excessive or uncontrollable, outside of your concerns about your face and jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m trying to think. I mean, I worry about deadlines and whether clients are happy, but that feels like normal architect worry, if there is such a thing. Buildings have so many moving parts—permits, materials, people changing their minds after they’ve already approved something…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. Outside of my face and jaw, I don’t think there’s anything that feels that uncontrollable in the same way. I can get stressed, definitely, but I haven’t noticed some separate worry that takes over like this does.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life, besides your appearance, where you feel like you're excessively focused on details or flaws?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, in my work I notice flaws constantly—that’s part of the job, really. If a line is off or a material doesn’t sit right, it bothers me, but that feels… normal? Or at least normal for architects.\n\nMy husband jokes that I can spot a crooked picture frame from across a room, which is true, but I don’t think it takes over the way this does. Though now I’m thinking about this kitchen renovation we did years ago where the cabinet gaps were uneven and I kept pointing it out until everyone was sick of me—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… besides my appearance, I don’t know that there’s another area where it feels excessive in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your attention to detail at work is similar to how you focus on the details of your face?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but it’s different. At work, if I’m looking at a drawing or a model, the details have a purpose. Like, a line being off actually changes something—sorry, I’m thinking of this facade detail that drove me crazy once because the shadow gap was wrong.\n\nBut with my face, it’s like I can’t let it go. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo or reflection. Even when people say I look fine, it feels so obvious to me. And then I start thinking, how can I stand in front of clients like this? That’s when I avoid presentations or don’t want to meet people. So… no, it’s not really the same. It feels much more stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your focus on the details of your face is similar to how you focus on details in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s… similar in that I notice tiny things, I guess. Like if a line is off in a drawing, I’ll see it right away—actually there was this stair detail once that nobody else cared about and I couldn’t stop pointing at it, which probably drove everyone mad.\n\nBut my face feels different. That’s why I’m here, really. I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo, every reflection. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” it doesn’t land. It feels so obvious to me that I start thinking everyone must be noticing it, and then I don’t want to give presentations or meet clients. So… yes and no. The attention to detail is familiar, but this is much more consuming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be this bothered by small imperfections in other areas of your life if they weren't related to your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I can get annoyed by things—like if a tile line is off, or a drawing has some tiny mistake, and then I’ll think about it longer than maybe I should. My husband would say I’m impossible about that sort of thing.\n\nBut… I’m here because of my face. Because I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s in every photo, every reflection, even a dark window sometimes—sorry, that sounds dramatic, but it’s true. And when people tell me I look fine, it doesn’t really help, because to me it feels so obvious.\n\nWith other imperfections, I might be irritated. With this, I start avoiding presentations and clients. So no, I don’t think other small flaws would bother me in the same way if they weren’t on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d call it excessive. I mean, I do notice things being off—especially in work things, like if a drawing isn’t lined up or a material sample looks wrong under the light. Architects are trained to notice that, you know? My first boss used to say if you miss a millimeter on paper it becomes a whole problem on site, which… I suppose stuck with me.

But outside of work? I don’t know. I can get fussy, maybe. Like if something in the house is crooked I’ll straighten it without thinking, or if a task isn’t done properly I might redo it. But I don’t know if that’s “excessively critical” or just… me being particular. My husband would probably have an opinion on that.

My husband would probably say I fuss over things, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t noticed anything that feels separate from the face and jaw thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationship with your husband?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because it feels like everything is affected lately. My husband sees me getting ready and then not leaving, or making us late because I’m stuck looking at my face, and then I get upset and he doesn’t know what to say.\n\nIt’s not just at home, though—like, I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I couldn’t face being seen, and I’ve been missing those award networking events too. Those things sound work-related, but then I come home tense and ashamed, and it spills over. Project deadlines are slipping, family gatherings are falling apart… so yes, it’s definitely affecting us, even if I don’t always admit that to him.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other thoughts or worries that you feel are excessive or uncontrollable, outside of your concerns about your face and jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m trying to think. I mean, I worry about deadlines and whether clients are happy, but that feels like normal architect worry, if there is such a thing. Buildings have so many moving parts—permits, materials, people changing their minds after they’ve already approved something…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. Outside of my face and jaw, I don’t think there’s anything that feels that uncontrollable in the same way. I can get stressed, definitely, but I haven’t noticed some separate worry that takes over like this does.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life, besides your appearance, where you feel like you're excessively focused on details or flaws?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, in my work I notice flaws constantly—that’s part of the job, really. If a line is off or a material doesn’t sit right, it bothers me, but that feels… normal? Or at least normal for architects.\n\nMy husband jokes that I can spot a crooked picture frame from across a room, which is true, but I don’t think it takes over the way this does. Though now I’m thinking about this kitchen renovation we did years ago where the cabinet gaps were uneven and I kept pointing it out until everyone was sick of me—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… besides my appearance, I don’t know that there’s another area where it feels excessive in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your attention to detail at work is similar to how you focus on the details of your face?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but it’s different. At work, if I’m looking at a drawing or a model, the details have a purpose. Like, a line being off actually changes something—sorry, I’m thinking of this facade detail that drove me crazy once because the shadow gap was wrong.\n\nBut with my face, it’s like I can’t let it go. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo or reflection. Even when people say I look fine, it feels so obvious to me. And then I start thinking, how can I stand in front of clients like this? That’s when I avoid presentations or don’t want to meet people. So… no, it’s not really the same. It feels much more stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your focus on the details of your face is similar to how you focus on details in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s… similar in that I notice tiny things, I guess. Like if a line is off in a drawing, I’ll see it right away—actually there was this stair detail once that nobody else cared about and I couldn’t stop pointing at it, which probably drove everyone mad.\n\nBut my face feels different. That’s why I’m here, really. I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo, every reflection. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” it doesn’t land. It feels so obvious to me that I start thinking everyone must be noticing it, and then I don’t want to give presentations or meet clients. So… yes and no. The attention to detail is familiar, but this is much more consuming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be this bothered by small imperfections in other areas of your life if they weren't related to your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I can get annoyed by things—like if a tile line is off, or a drawing has some tiny mistake, and then I’ll think about it longer than maybe I should. My husband would say I’m impossible about that sort of thing.\n\nBut… I’m here because of my face. Because I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s in every photo, every reflection, even a dark window sometimes—sorry, that sounds dramatic, but it’s true. And when people tell me I look fine, it doesn’t really help, because to me it feels so obvious.\n\nWith other imperfections, I might be irritated. With this, I start avoiding presentations and clients. So no, I don’t think other small flaws would bother me in the same way if they weren’t on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being excessively critical of small imperfections in things other than your appearance, such as objects or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive. I mean, I do notice things being off—especially in work things, like if a drawing isn’t lined up or a material sample looks wrong under the light. Architects are trained to notice that, you know? My first boss used to say if you miss a millimeter on paper it becomes a whole problem on site, which… I suppose stuck with me.\n\nBut outside of work? I don’t know. I can get fussy, maybe. Like if something in the house is crooked I’ll straighten it without thinking, or if a task isn’t done properly I might redo it. But I don’t know if that’s “excessively critical” or just… me being particular. My husband would probably have an opinion on that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My husband would probably have an opinion on that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your tendency to notice and focus on small imperfections is something that applies to many areas of your life, not just your face?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / architect / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do notice things, yes—probably more than some people. Like in a room, if something’s uneven, or in a plan if a line doesn’t quite meet, my eye goes there. But then again that’s also just… my job, isn’t it? You spend years being told to see those things.

Sorry, I’m wandering. With my face it feels different, but if you’re asking whether I’m like this everywhere in life… I don’t know. Maybe in some areas. I haven’t really thought about it that way. My husband would probably say yes before I would.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just… I can’t stop noticing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s in every photo, every reflection, even in windows when I’m walking by—actually, the glass in office buildings is the worst, which is ridiculous because I work around buildings all day.\n\nPeople keep telling me I look fine, but I don’t know how they don’t see it. It feels so obvious to me. And it’s gotten to where I’m avoiding presentations and meeting clients, which is a problem because that’s a big part of my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your reflection throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. Before work especially, or if we’re going to some family thing, I can lose hours in the bathroom mirror. I’ll check one side, then the other, then take pictures on my phone from different angles because the mirror feels like it lies sometimes, but then the photos look worse.\n\nAnd then I’ll redo my makeup, adjust my scarf, take it off, put it back on… my husband will be waiting by the door and I’ll ask him, “Does my face look lopsided today?” He always says no, but I don’t really believe him. Then sometimes I end up looking at other women’s photos online, comparing mine to theirs, which is not helpful, I know, but I still do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking rituals or will you feel anxious if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t think of it like a ritual exactly, but if I try not to check, I get really tense. Like before work, or if there’s a family event, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to look once and leave, but then I’m in the bathroom for ages—checking the mirror, taking phone pictures from different angles, redoing my makeup, changing my scarf.\n\nAnd my husband will be waiting, and I’ll ask him again if my face looks lopsided, even though I already know what he’s going to say. Then sometimes I end up scrolling through photos of other women, comparing, which is stupid because it just makes me feel worse. But if I don’t do it, it’s like I can’t settle. I keep thinking, what if it really looks bad today and I didn’t catch it?\n\nDoctor: Is it only your face and jaw that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my face… the mouth and jaw area, that droop or unevenness. That’s the thing I can’t stop seeing. In photos, reflections, even like… walking past glass buildings, which I notice constantly because of work, and then I’m supposed to go into a meeting and act normal.\n\nI know people say I look fine, but it feels so obvious to me. Like clients must be looking at it when I’m talking. So I’ve started avoiding presentations when I can, or trying to stay out of client meetings. I don’t really focus on other parts of my body the same way—it’s this part of my face that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face could ever look perfectly even and symmetrical to you, or do you believe it's inherently flawed in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I think that’s the problem. Part of me keeps thinking if I just catch the right angle or fix my makeup the right way, maybe it’ll look even. But then every photo, every reflection, I see that droop around my mouth and jaw again.\n\nIt feels built in, like it’s just my face. And I know people say I look fine, but I can’t unsee it. I’ll be in a meeting, or even thinking about a presentation, and all I can imagine is clients staring at that uneven part while I’m talking. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients when I can. It just feels too obvious.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about other things being uneven or flawed besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I notice little things sometimes, like anyone does, but it doesn’t grab me the same way. It’s this droop around my mouth and my jaw, the unevenness there. I see it in every photo, every reflection, even when I’m just passing a window.\n\nAnd then I’m supposed to go meet a client and talk about a design like I’m not thinking the whole time that they’re staring at it. I know people say I look fine, my husband says it, but it just feels so obvious to me. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and client meetings when I can. It’s really that part of my face that I can’t stop seeing.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw affected your relationships with friends and family, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, it has. I try to tell myself it’s mainly work, but then it spills over. Like there was this awards thing—architectural awards, lots of people standing around with drinks and pretending not to judge each other’s shoes, you know—and I just couldn’t go. I kept thinking everyone would be looking at my face when I talked.\n\nAnd family gatherings… I’ve started missing those or leaving things to the last minute until it all falls apart. My husband gets frustrated because we’ll have plans and then I can’t make myself get ready, or I’ll keep checking my face and suddenly we’re late or not going.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I even cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute, which wasn’t family, I know, but it all feels connected. My deadlines are slipping, people are upset with me, and at home it’s like everything is revolving around whether I can stand being seen that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that even if someone proved to you that your face is symmetrical, you would still see it as uneven?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, even when people tell me it looks fine, or I try to convince myself a photo is just lighting, I still see that droop around my mouth and jaw. It’s like my eyes go straight to it.\n\nAnd I know symmetry is one of those things architects talk about all the time—balance, proportion, all that—and maybe that makes me worse about it, I don’t know. But if someone showed me measurements or something, I think I’d still look in the mirror and feel like, no, it’s uneven. It just feels so obvious to me that I can’t stop checking for it, and then I don’t want clients or people in presentations looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw started to affect your overall quality of life or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I keep thinking of it as “just” work, but it’s not. I mean, work is where it shows up in the biggest, most humiliating ways—like I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face being seen.\n\nAnd then there are these award-related networking things I’m supposed to go to, and I know they matter. You see people, you make contacts, you pretend to enjoy tiny appetizers… but I’ve been missing those too. I’ll get dressed and then start looking at my face and suddenly I can’t go.\n\nAnd it’s not staying neatly in one box. My project deadlines are slipping, and family gatherings are starting to fall apart because I either avoid them or make us late or just don’t go. It feels like everything gets planned around whether I can stand people looking at my face that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having intrusive thoughts or compulsions to repeat certain behaviors that are not related to your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really, I don’t think. I mean, unless you count the checking, but that’s obviously about my face. Other than that, I don’t think I have rituals or anything.\n\nI do get stuck on things sometimes, but I’ve always been particular—architecture sort of rewards that, you know, checking lines and details and whether something is level. My husband would probably say I fuss over things, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t noticed anything that feels separate from the face and jaw thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationship with your husband?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because it feels like everything is affected lately. My husband sees me getting ready and then not leaving, or making us late because I’m stuck looking at my face, and then I get upset and he doesn’t know what to say.\n\nIt’s not just at home, though—like, I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I couldn’t face being seen, and I’ve been missing those award networking events too. Those things sound work-related, but then I come home tense and ashamed, and it spills over. Project deadlines are slipping, family gatherings are falling apart… so yes, it’s definitely affecting us, even if I don’t always admit that to him.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other thoughts or worries that you feel are excessive or uncontrollable, outside of your concerns about your face and jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m trying to think. I mean, I worry about deadlines and whether clients are happy, but that feels like normal architect worry, if there is such a thing. Buildings have so many moving parts—permits, materials, people changing their minds after they’ve already approved something…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. Outside of my face and jaw, I don’t think there’s anything that feels that uncontrollable in the same way. I can get stressed, definitely, but I haven’t noticed some separate worry that takes over like this does.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life, besides your appearance, where you feel like you're excessively focused on details or flaws?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, in my work I notice flaws constantly—that’s part of the job, really. If a line is off or a material doesn’t sit right, it bothers me, but that feels… normal? Or at least normal for architects.\n\nMy husband jokes that I can spot a crooked picture frame from across a room, which is true, but I don’t think it takes over the way this does. Though now I’m thinking about this kitchen renovation we did years ago where the cabinet gaps were uneven and I kept pointing it out until everyone was sick of me—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… besides my appearance, I don’t know that there’s another area where it feels excessive in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your attention to detail at work is similar to how you focus on the details of your face?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but it’s different. At work, if I’m looking at a drawing or a model, the details have a purpose. Like, a line being off actually changes something—sorry, I’m thinking of this facade detail that drove me crazy once because the shadow gap was wrong.\n\nBut with my face, it’s like I can’t let it go. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo or reflection. Even when people say I look fine, it feels so obvious to me. And then I start thinking, how can I stand in front of clients like this? That’s when I avoid presentations or don’t want to meet people. So… no, it’s not really the same. It feels much more stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your focus on the details of your face is similar to how you focus on details in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s… similar in that I notice tiny things, I guess. Like if a line is off in a drawing, I’ll see it right away—actually there was this stair detail once that nobody else cared about and I couldn’t stop pointing at it, which probably drove everyone mad.\n\nBut my face feels different. That’s why I’m here, really. I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo, every reflection. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” it doesn’t land. It feels so obvious to me that I start thinking everyone must be noticing it, and then I don’t want to give presentations or meet clients. So… yes and no. The attention to detail is familiar, but this is much more consuming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be this bothered by small imperfections in other areas of your life if they weren't related to your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I can get annoyed by things—like if a tile line is off, or a drawing has some tiny mistake, and then I’ll think about it longer than maybe I should. My husband would say I’m impossible about that sort of thing.\n\nBut… I’m here because of my face. Because I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s in every photo, every reflection, even a dark window sometimes—sorry, that sounds dramatic, but it’s true. And when people tell me I look fine, it doesn’t really help, because to me it feels so obvious.\n\nWith other imperfections, I might be irritated. With this, I start avoiding presentations and clients. So no, I don’t think other small flaws would bother me in the same way if they weren’t on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being excessively critical of small imperfections in things other than your appearance, such as objects or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive. I mean, I do notice things being off—especially in work things, like if a drawing isn’t lined up or a material sample looks wrong under the light. Architects are trained to notice that, you know? My first boss used to say if you miss a millimeter on paper it becomes a whole problem on site, which… I suppose stuck with me.\n\nBut outside of work? I don’t know. I can get fussy, maybe. Like if something in the house is crooked I’ll straighten it without thinking, or if a task isn’t done properly I might redo it. But I don’t know if that’s “excessively critical” or just… me being particular. My husband would probably have an opinion on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to notice and focus on small imperfections is something that applies to many areas of your life, not just your face?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do notice things, yes—probably more than some people. Like in a room, if something’s uneven, or in a plan if a line doesn’t quite meet, my eye goes there. But then again that’s also just… my job, isn’t it? You spend years being told to see those things.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. With my face it feels different, but if you’re asking whether I’m like this everywhere in life… I don’t know. Maybe in some areas. I haven’t really thought about it that way. My husband would probably say yes before I would.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F45.2", "F42.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

My husband would say I’m impossible about that sort of thing.\n\nBut… I’m here because of my face. Because I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s in every photo, every reflection, even a dark window sometimes—sorry, that sounds dramatic, but it’s true. With this, I start avoiding presentations and clients. So no, I don’t think other small flaws would bother me in the same way if they weren’t on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being excessively critical of small imperfections in things other than your appearance, such as objects or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive. I mean, I do notice things being off—especially in work things, like if a drawing isn’t lined up or a material sample looks wrong under the light. Architects are trained to notice that, you know? My first boss used to say if you miss a millimeter on paper it becomes a whole problem on site, which… I suppose stuck with me.\n\nBut outside of work? I don’t know. I can get fussy, maybe. Like if something in the house is crooked I’ll straighten it without thinking, or if a task isn’t done properly I might redo it. But I don’t know if that’s “excessively critical” or just… me being particular. My husband would probably have an opinion on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to notice and focus on small imperfections is something that applies to many areas of your life, not just your face?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Yes, I think that’s probably fair. I mean, at work it’s useful—if a line is off or a proportion doesn’t sit right, I can catch it before it becomes a bigger issue. That part of me has helped me, honestly.

But with my face it’s… it doesn’t feel useful. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s like my eye goes straight to it in every photo, every reflection—even when I’m just passing a shop window, which sounds ridiculous because I’m supposed to be thinking about errands or a client meeting, not my jaw.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just… I can’t stop noticing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s in every photo, every reflection, even in windows when I’m walking by—actually, the glass in office buildings is the worst, which is ridiculous because I work around buildings all day.\n\nPeople keep telling me I look fine, but I don’t know how they don’t see it. It feels so obvious to me. And it’s gotten to where I’m avoiding presentations and meeting clients, which is a problem because that’s a big part of my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your reflection throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. Before work especially, or if we’re going to some family thing, I can lose hours in the bathroom mirror. That’s the thing I can’t stop seeing. I don’t really focus on other parts of my body the same way—it’s this part of my face that gets stuck in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face could ever look perfectly even and symmetrical to you, or do you believe it's inherently flawed in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I think that’s the problem. That’s why I’ve been avoiding presentations and clients when I can. It just feels too obvious.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about other things being uneven or flawed besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I notice little things sometimes, like anyone does, but it doesn’t grab me the same way. It’s this droop around my mouth and my jaw, the unevenness there. I try to tell myself it’s mainly work, but then it spills over. Like there was this awards thing—architectural awards, lots of people standing around with drinks and pretending not to judge each other’s shoes, you know—and I just couldn’t go. I kept thinking everyone would be looking at my face when I talked.\n\nAnd family gatherings… I’ve started missing those or leaving things to the last minute until it all falls apart. My husband gets frustrated because we’ll have plans and then I can’t make myself get ready, or I’ll keep checking my face and suddenly we’re late or not going.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I even cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute, which wasn’t family, I know, but it all feels connected. My deadlines are slipping, people are upset with me, and at home it’s like everything is revolving around whether I can stand being seen that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that even if someone proved to you that your face is symmetrical, you would still see it as uneven?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, even when people tell me it looks fine, or I try to convince myself a photo is just lighting, I still see that droop around my mouth and jaw. It’s like my eyes go straight to it.\n\nAnd I know symmetry is one of those things architects talk about all the time—balance, proportion, all that—and maybe that makes me worse about it, I don’t know. But if someone showed me measurements or something, I think I’d still look in the mirror and feel like, no, it’s uneven. It just feels so obvious to me that I can’t stop checking for it, and then I don’t want clients or people in presentations looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and jaw started to affect your overall quality of life or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I keep thinking of it as “just” work, but it’s not. I mean, work is where it shows up in the biggest, most humiliating ways—like I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I just couldn’t face being seen.\n\nAnd then there are these award-related networking things I’m supposed to go to, and I know they matter. You see people, you make contacts, you pretend to enjoy tiny appetizers… but I’ve been missing those too. I’ll get dressed and then start looking at my face and suddenly I can’t go.\n\nAnd it’s not staying neatly in one box. It feels like everything gets planned around whether I can stand people looking at my face that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having intrusive thoughts or compulsions to repeat certain behaviors that are not related to your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really, I don’t think. I mean, unless you count the checking, but that’s obviously about my face. I haven’t noticed anything that feels separate from the face and jaw thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationship with your husband?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because it feels like everything is affected lately. My husband sees me getting ready and then not leaving, or making us late because I’m stuck looking at my face, and then I get upset and he doesn’t know what to say.\n\nIt’s not just at home, though—like, I cancelled a major client presentation at the last minute because I couldn’t face being seen, and I’ve been missing those award networking events too. Project deadlines are slipping, family gatherings are falling apart… so yes, it’s definitely affecting us, even if I don’t always admit that to him.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other thoughts or worries that you feel are excessive or uncontrollable, outside of your concerns about your face and jaw?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m trying to think. I mean, I worry about deadlines and whether clients are happy, but that feels like normal architect worry, if there is such a thing. Buildings have so many moving parts—permits, materials, people changing their minds after they’ve already approved something…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. Outside of my face and jaw, I don’t think there’s anything that feels that uncontrollable in the same way. I can get stressed, definitely, but I haven’t noticed some separate worry that takes over like this does.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life, besides your appearance, where you feel like you're excessively focused on details or flaws?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, in my work I notice flaws constantly—that’s part of the job, really. If a line is off or a material doesn’t sit right, it bothers me, but that feels… normal? Or at least normal for architects.\n\nMy husband jokes that I can spot a crooked picture frame from across a room, which is true, but I don’t think it takes over the way this does. Though now I’m thinking about this kitchen renovation we did years ago where the cabinet gaps were uneven and I kept pointing it out until everyone was sick of me—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… besides my appearance, I don’t know that there’s another area where it feels excessive in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your attention to detail at work is similar to how you focus on the details of your face?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but it’s different. At work, if I’m looking at a drawing or a model, the details have a purpose. I keep seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo or reflection. Even when people say I look fine, it feels so obvious to me. And then I start thinking, how can I stand in front of clients like this? That’s when I avoid presentations or don’t want to meet people. So… no, it’s not really the same. Like if a line is off in a drawing, I’ll see it right away—actually there was this stair detail once that nobody else cared about and I couldn’t stop pointing at it, which probably drove everyone mad.\n\nBut my face feels different. That’s why I’m here, really. I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw, in every photo, every reflection. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” it doesn’t land. It feels so obvious to me that I start thinking everyone must be noticing it, and then I don’t want to give presentations or meet clients. So… yes and no. The attention to detail is familiar, but this is much more consuming.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be this bothered by small imperfections in other areas of your life if they weren't related to your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I can get annoyed by things—like if a tile line is off, or a drawing has some tiny mistake, and then I’ll think about it longer than maybe I should. My husband would say I’m impossible about that sort of thing.\n\nBut… I’m here because of my face. Because I can’t stop seeing this droop, this unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It’s in every photo, every reflection, even a dark window sometimes—sorry, that sounds dramatic, but it’s true. And when people tell me I look fine, it doesn’t really help, because to me it feels so obvious.\n\nWith other imperfections, I might be irritated. With this, I start avoiding presentations and clients. So no, I don’t think other small flaws would bother me in the same way if they weren’t on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being excessively critical of small imperfections in things other than your appearance, such as objects or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it excessive. I mean, I do notice things being off—especially in work things, like if a drawing isn’t lined up or a material sample looks wrong under the light. Architects are trained to notice that, you know? My first boss used to say if you miss a millimeter on paper it becomes a whole problem on site, which… I suppose stuck with me.\n\nBut outside of work? I don’t know. I can get fussy, maybe. Like if something in the house is crooked I’ll straighten it without thinking, or if a task isn’t done properly I might redo it. But I don’t know if that’s “excessively critical” or just… me being particular. My husband would probably have an opinion on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to notice and focus on small imperfections is something that applies to many areas of your life, not just your face?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure.
